F. MISCELLANEOUS ISSUES 


F.l Views of the other interested parties 


5l. The following miscellaneous submissions have been made by the other interested parties: 


a. 


Sufficiency of evidence and termination of the investigation: The application does not contain 
sufficient evidence of dumping, injury and causal link as required under Rule 5(3), and rests on 
assumptions and historical findings. The burden lies on the domestic industry to establish 
likelihood through positive evidence. The investigation should be terminated under Rule 4(b). 


. Period of investigation: The period of investigation does not coincide with the financial year and 


has been selected to project an increase in imports, whereas imports declined in 2024-25. It amounts 
to an improper comparison of periods. 


. Source of import data: The application relies on import data from a private source, which is not 


reliable. The Authority should call for and rely on official import data in terms of its Manual of 
Operating Practices. 


. Post-POI data: A sunset review is forward-looking, and the Manual recognises post-POI 


information. Trade flows, freight rates and exchange rates have changed after the period of 
investigation. The Authority should call for and examine post-POI information. 


. Anti-circumvention history and writ proceedings: The earlier anti-circumvention proceedings 


cannot be attributed to Guangdong Fuwa. HDTPL states that the charges framed against it by the 
customs authorities were dropped by an Order-in-Original dated 27th June 208, and that its writ 
petition was withdrawn thereafter as nothing survived in it. 


. Estoppel: The applicant’s annual report attributes the slowdown in market growth to weaker 


industrial activity and the suspension of infrastructure projects. The applicant is estopped from 
attributing any reduction in its income or profit to imports. 


. Abuse of the mechanism, costs and recovery of duty: The applicant has derived financial gain 


from the duty. If it is found ineligible to constitute domestic industry, the earlier measures are void 
ab initio, and the duty paid by HDTPL should be recovered from the applicant with exemplary costs. 
The matter should be referred to the competition authorities. 


. Exemption for HDTPL: HDTPL, as the authorised distributor of Shandong Darong Machinery Co., 


Ltd. and Shijiazhuang Aotong Machinery Manufacturing Co., Ltd., should be allowed to import the 
PUC from these producers without payment of anti-dumping duty. 


F.2 Views of the domestic industry 


52. The following miscellaneous submissions have been made by the domestic industry: 


a. 


Sufficiency of evidence and termination of the investigation: The present proceeding is a sunset 
review under Section 9A(5) and Rule 23(/B). Rule 23(3) does not apply Rules 5 and 4 to a 
review. Rule 4 provides for termination of an original investigation and cannot be imported into a 
review, and the initiation test is not to be re-applied at this stage. Reliance is placed on Indian 
Metal and Ferro Alloys Ltd. v. Designated Authority [2007 (24) E.L.T. 3 (Del.)]. 

Period of investigation: The period of investigation is a complete period of twelve months, which 
is the normal period under the Rules, and need not coincide with the financial year. HDTPL 
compares it with 2024-25, with which it overlaps by six months. 

Source of import data: Transaction-wise official data was not made available to the applicant 
despite a request. The applicant relied on the best information available to it, which the practice of 
the Authority permits. The Authority obtains official data for its determination. 

Post-POI data: Post-POI information may be considered where material, but the Manual does not 
make it a precondition to a determination. The record already contains current information. 


[भाग [---खण्ड ]] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 47 


6. Anti-circumvention history and writ proceedings: The withdrawal of the writ petition is not 
relied upon as a finding on merits. The material fact is the anti-circumvention investigation and the 
final findings dated l4th September 202, which show the commercial incentive to retain access to 
the Indian market despite the duty. 

f. Estoppel: A statement in an annual report on general market conditions is not an admission 
regarding dumping, and estoppel cannot prevent a statutory examination. The existence of other 
factors only calls for a non-attribution analysis. 


F.3 Examination by the Authority 


53. 


54. 


55. 


56. 


57. 


58. 


Sufficiency of evidence and termination of the investigation 

The present investigation is a sunset review under Section 9A(5) of the Act and Rule 23(/B) of the 
Rules, initiated on a duly substantiated request made by the domestic industry. Rule 23(3) makes Rules 
6, 7, 8, 9, 0, l, 6, 7, 8, I9 and 20 applicable mutatis mutandis to a review. Rule 5, which governs 
the initiation of an original investigation, and Rule 4, which provides for its termination, are not 
among the provisions so applied. The prayer for termination under Rule 4(b) is therefore 
misconceived. The determination in the review will rest on the evidence gathered in the course of the 
investigation, which is disclosed in these final findings. 

Period of investigation 

The period of investigation is a continuous period of twelve months and was less than six months old 
on the date of initiation. There is no requirement that it coincide with a financial year, and it is the 
consistent practice of the Authority to examine trends over three preceding financial years and the 
period of investigation. The partial overlap between 2024-25 and the period of investigation is inherent 
in that practice and has been kept in view. 

Source of import data 

The applicant disclosed in its application that it had relied on a secondary source and filed a copy of its 
request for official data. The Authority has not relied on that data. It has called for transaction-wise data 
from DG Systems and has relied upon the same for its analysis. The official data shows a higher 
volume of imports in each period than that reported in the application. 


Post-POI data 

The Authority agrees that post-POI developments may be relevant in a sunset review, and has examined 
the DG Systems data for the period after the period of investigation. Imports of the subject goods from 
the subject country have continued after the period of investigation at comparable volumes and prices. 
Guangdong Fuwa has referred in general terms to changes in freight rates, exchange rates and trade 
flows, but has not placed on record any post-POI information on its own capacity, production, export 
volumes or prices, nor any evidence of how the developments referred to have altered the likelihood of 
dumping or injury. 

Anti-circumvention history and writ proceedings 

The Authority notes the chronology of the customs proceedings as stated by HDTPL, and that the 
domestic industry does not rely on the withdrawal of the writ petition as a finding on merits. Those 
proceedings are not the basis of any fact disclosed in these final findings. The final findings in the anti- 
circumvention investigation and the consequent extension of the duty stand on their own record and are 
not the subject of this review. The Authority does not attribute those findings individually to 
Guangdong Fuwa. The likelihood of continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury has been 
examined on the present record in these final findings. 


Estoppel 


The statement in the applicant’s annual report concerns the general slowdown in market growth. It is 
not an admission regarding dumping or its effects, and no question of estoppel arises against a statutory 


48 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


59. 


60. 


examination that the Authority is required to carry out on the evidence before it. The movement in 
demand has been taken into account in the non-attribution analysis. 

Abuse of the mechanism, costs and recovery of duty 

The Authority’s functions under the Act and the Rules are to investigate and to make its 
recommendation to the Central Government. The Rules do not confer on the Authority any power to 
award costs or compensation, to direct recovery or refund of duty paid under notifications of the 
Central Government, or to declare earlier notifications void. The findings in the earlier investigations 
have attained finality and are not open to re-examination in this review. In any case, these prayers are 
premised on the applicant being ineligible to constitute domestic industry, which, as noted above, is not 
the position. 

Exemption for HDTPL 

Anti-dumping duty is determined with reference to the producer and exporter of the goods and not the 
importer. An individual rate is determined for a producer or exporter that participates in the 
investigation and furnishes complete and verifiable information. Neither of the producers named by 
HDTPL has filed a questionnaire response in the present review. The Rules do not contemplate an 
importer-specific exemption, and the request cannot be acceded to. 


G. NORMAL VALUE, EXPORT PRICE AND DUMPING MARGIN 


G.l Views of the other interested parties 


6l. 


The other interested parties have submitted as follows with regard to the determination of normal value, 
export price and dumping margin: 

a. Non-market economy treatment: Section ]5(a)(ii) of China’s Protocol of Accession to the 
WTO has expired, and India no longer has a legal basis to compute normal value by a non- 
market economy methodology. 

b. Dumping margin: The dumping margin alleged by the domestic industry is only a claim. 
Margins of earlier proceedings relate to those periods. The dumping margin should be 
determined on verified, exporter-specific information. 

c. Transactions of HDTPL: There is no dumping during the period of investigation. On a 
comparison of domestic sales invoices of one of HDTPL’s suppliers with the export invoices 
of another, the dumping margin is negative. There is also no dumping as imports have not 
increased. 


G.2 Views of the domestic industry 


62. 


The domestic industry has submitted as follows with regard to the determination of normal value, 

export price and dumping margin: 

a. Non-market economy treatment: China PR should be treated as a non-market economy. The 
expiry of Section l5(a)(ii) did not confer market economy status, and no Chinese producer has 
shown that market economy conditions prevail. Normal value should be determined under 
paragraph 7 of Annexure I. 

b. Basis of normal value: South Africa is an appropriate market economy third country, on the basis 
of the landed price of the applicant’s exports to that country. Alternatively, normal value should be 
constructed on the cost of production in India with reasonable profit. 

c. Dumping margin: The dumping margin is positive and significant, and has remained so in the 
original investigation, the first sunset review and the present review. 

d. Transactions of HDTPL: HDTPL compares invoices of two different producers and of different 
periods, the normal value invoices being of a period after the period of investigation. The selected 
transactions of one importer cannot establish the absence of dumping from the subject country. 


[भाग [खण्ड [] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 49 


G.3 Examination by the Authority 


G.3.lDetermination of normal value 

63. Article 5 of China’s Accession Protocol to the WTO provides as follows: 
“Article VI of the 04777994, the Agreement on Implementation of Article VI of the General 
Agreement on Tariffs and Trade /994 (“Anti-Dumping Agreement”) and the SCM 
Agreement shall apply in proceedings involving imports of Chinese origin into a WTO 
Member consistent with the following: 
(a) In determining price comparability under Article VI of the GATT /994 and the Anti- 
Dumping Agreement, the importing WTO Member shall use either Chinese prices or costs 
for the industry under investigation or a methodology that is not based on a strict 
comparison with domestic prices or costs in China based on the following rules: 
(i) If the producers under investigation can clearly show that market economy conditions 
prevail in the industry producing the like product with regard to the manufacture, 
production and sale of that product, the importing WTO Member shall use Chinese prices or 
costs for the industry under investigation in determining price comparability; 
(ii) The importing WTO Member may use a methodology that is not based on a strict 
comparison with domestic prices or costs in China if the producers under investigation 
cannot clearly show that market economy conditions prevail in the industry producing the 
like product with regard to manufacture, production and sale of that product. 


(d) Once China has established, under the national law of the importing WTO Member, that 
it is a market economy, the provisions of subparagraph (a) shall be terminated provided that 
the importing Member’s national law contains market economy criteria as of the date of 
accession. In any event, the provisions of subparagraph (a)(ii) shall expire 73 years after the 
date of accession. In addition, should China establish, pursuant to the national law of the 
importing WTO Member, that market economy conditions prevail in a particular industry or 
sector, the non-market economy provisions of subparagraph (a) shall no longer apply to that 
industry or sector.” 

64. It is noted that while the provision contained in Article ]5(a)(ii) has expired, Article 5(a)(i), read with 
paragraph 8 of Annexure I to the Rules, requires the producers under investigation to show that market 
economy conditions prevail in the industry producing the like product. Guangdong Fuwa has neither 
claimed market economy treatment nor furnished the information required to rebut the presumption 
under paragraph 8(2) of Annexure I. No other producer has responded. The normal value is accordingly 
required to be determined in terms of paragraph 7 of Annexure I, which reads as under: 

“In case of imports from non-market economy countries, normal value shall be determined 
on the basis of the price or constructed value in the market economy third country, or the 
price from such a third country to other countries, including India or where it is not 
possible, or on any other reasonable basis, including the price actually paid or payable in 
India for the like product, duly adjusted, if necessary, to include a reasonable profit margin. 
An appropriate market economy third country shall be selected by the designated authority 
in a reasonable manner, keeping in view the level of development of the country concerned 
and the product in question, and due account shall be taken of any reliable information 
made available at the time of selection. Accounts shall be taken within time limits, where 


50 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


65. 


66. 


67. 


appropriate, of the investigation made in any similar matter in respect of any other market 
economy third country. The parties to the investigation shall be informed without any 
unreasonable delay the aforesaid selection of the market economy third country and shall be 


J 


given a reasonable period of time to offer their comments.’ 


The domestic industry has proposed South Africa as an appropriate market-economy third country, 
stating that it exported substantial quantities of the subject goods to that market during the POI and that 
the landed prices of such exports could represent prevailing prices in South Africa. However, as noted 
at initiation, the applicant has not substantiated either the suitability of South Africa as the appropriate 
third country or the use of its own landed export prices as representative prices in that market. No 
reliable supporting evidence of domestic prices or constructed value of the like article in South Africa 
has been furnished. 

The Authority has examined the alternatives prescribed under paragraph 7 of Annexure I in sequence. 
First, the price or constructed value in an appropriate market-economy third country could not be 
adopted, as the selection of South Africa and the proposed price basis were not substantiated. Second, 
reliable information on the price of the like article exported from an appropriate market-economy third 
country to other countries, including India, is not available. Accordingly, neither of the first two 
alternatives could be applied. The Authority has therefore adopted the remaining reasonable basis under 
paragraph 7 and determined normal value on the basis of the price payable in India, constructed from 
the cost of production of the like article in India, with appropriate adjustments for selling, general and 
administrative expenses and a reasonable profit. 

HDTPL’s proposed calculation cannot be accepted, as it compares post-POI domestic sales invoices of 
a non-participating producer, which has not claimed market-economy treatment, with export invoices of 
a different producer. Further, the absence of an increase in import volumes does not establish the 
absence of dumping, which is determined by comparing normal value and export price.

H. ASSESSMENT OF INJURY AND CAUSAL LINK 


H.l Views of the other interested parties 
73. The following submissions have been made by the other interested parties with regard to injury and 
causal link: 

a. Nature of the examination: The burden lies on the domestic industry to establish, through positive 
evidence, that expiry of the duty is likely to lead to continuation or recurrence of dumping and 
injury. The claims of injury are speculative. 

b. Volume effect: Imports from China PR have increased only marginally over the injury period, while 
demand and the sales of the domestic industry and of the other domestic producers have increased at 
a much higher rate. Imports have fluctuated and have declined over the longer term. There is no 
volume effect. 

c. Price effect: The landed price of imports has remained stable and the net sales realisation of the 
domestic industry has increased. Price undercutting without any adverse movement in sales 
realisation does not establish injury. On HDTPL’s transactions, the landed value is above the non- 
injurious price. 

d. Economic parameters: Production, capacity utilisation, domestic and export sales, profits, cash 
profits, employment, wages and return on capital employed of the domestic industry have all 
improved over the injury period, and inventories have declined. The decline in productivity per 
employee is explained by the increase in the workforce. 

e. Financial performance of the applicant as a company: The total income and profit after tax of the 
applicant have grown substantially, it holds large cash balances, and it has expanded its capacity. 
Such performance is inconsistent with injury. Reliance is placed on the sunset review findings in 
Soda Ash from Turkey and Russia. 

f. Causal link and other factors: No causal link is established. The increase in interest and 
depreciation costs is independent of imports and must be examined separately. Other factors such as 
cyclical demand, technology, the quality and cost of component suppliers, operational inefficiency 
and intense competition from other domestic producers may have contributed to any injury. 


H.2 Views of the domestic industry 
74. The following submissions have been made by the domestic industry with regard to injury and causal 
link: 
a. Nature of the examination: The present proceeding is a sunset review and not a fresh original 
investigation. The question is whether dumping and injury are likely to continue or recur if the duty 


52 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


expires. Improvement in performance is what an effective duty is expected to achieve and cannot, 
by itself, show that the duty is no longer needed. 


b. Volume effect: Imports have continued throughout the injury period despite the duty and are above 
the level of the first sunset review. The Indian producers have sufficient capacity to meet the entire 
Indian demand. 


c. Price effect: The imports have undercut the prices of the domestic industry throughout the injury 
period, and their landed price has remained below the cost of sales and the non-injurious price of 
the domestic industry. 


d. Economic parameters: The interested parties rely selectively on the improvement over the base 
year. Production, sales, profits, cash profits, employment and productivity declined in the period of 
investigation as compared with the preceding year, the market share of the domestic industry 
declined, and substantial capacity remained idle. 


e. Financial performance of the applicant as a company: The figures relied upon by HDTPL are 
company-wide and include products other than the PUC and trading activities. The injury analysis 
under the Rules concerns the like article. 

f. Causal link and other factors: In a sunset review a causal link between likely dumping and likely 
injury need not be established afresh (US — Anti-Dumping Measures on Oil Country Tubular 
Goods from Mexico, WT/DS282/AB/R). The other factors named by HDTPL have not been 
quantified and, at most, call for a non-attribution analysis. 


H.3 Examination by the Authority 


75. 


70. 


Rule ] of the Rules read with Annexure II provides that an injury determination shall involve an 
examination of factors that may indicate injury to the domestic industry, “... taking into account all 
relevant facts, including the volume of dumped imports, their effect on prices in the domestic market for 
like articles and the consequent effect of such imports on domestic producers of such articles ...”. Ina 
sunset review, the Authority examines whether the domestic industry has continued to suffer injury 
with the duty in force and whether injury is likely to continue or recur if the duty is allowed to expire. 
The present state of the domestic industry is examined in this part, and the likelihood of continuation or 
recurrence of dumping and injury in the part that follows. 

The injury examination is required to be made for the like article. The total income, profit after tax and 
cash balances cited by HDTPL are those of the applicant company as a whole, which also deals in 
products other than the PUC. The Authority has relied upon the information of the domestic industry 
segregated for domestic sales of the PUC.

H.3.3Economic parameters of the domestic industry 


86. Annexure II to the Rules requires that the determination of injury shall involve an objective 


examination of the consequent impact of the imports on domestic producers of the like article, 


including an evaluation of all relevant economic factors and indices having a bearing on the state of the 


industry. The performance of the domestic industry has accordingly been examined over the injury 


period.

Ability to raise capital investment 

The domestic industry has earned profits and a positive return on capital employed throughout the

Magnitude of dumping margin 

The subject goods continue to be exported to India at dumped prices. The dumping margin is positive 
and significant. 

Factors affecting domestic prices 

The Indian producers together hold the predominant share of the market, and there are six producers of 
the like article in India. The prices of the domestic industry during the injury period have been 
determined by its costs and by competition among the domestic producers and with the duty-paid 
imports. 


Overall assessment 


96. The interested parties have emphasised the improvement in the parameters of the domestic industry 


over the base year, and the domestic industry has emphasised the decline in the period of investigation 
over the preceding year and the idle capacity. The Authority is required to make a holistic assessment 
of all parameters. On such an assessment, the information on record shows that, with the anti-dumping 
duty in force, the domestic industry has improved its production, sales and capacity utilisation over the 
injury period, has remained profitable and has earned a reasonable return on capital employed in the 
period of investigation. The domestic industry is thus not found to be suffering continued injury with 
the duty in force.

I. CAUSAL LINK AND NON-ATTRIBUTION ANALYSIS 


The Authority has examined whether the movement in the performance of the domestic industry 
during the injury period is attributable to the subject imports or to other known factors, having regard 
to the submissions of the interested parties. 


a. Volume and value of imports from third countries: There are no imports of the subject 
goods from countries other than the subject country. 


b. Contraction in demand and changes in the pattern of consumption: Demand increased in 
2023-24 and declined thereafter. The moderation in the volumes of the domestic industry after 
2023-24 coincides with the decline in demand. No change in the pattern of consumption has 
been shown. 


८. Conditions of competition and trade restrictive practices: The sales and market share of 
the other Indian producers have increased over the injury period, while the market share of the 
domestic industry has declined. Competition among the domestic producers has thus been a 
factor in the decline in the market share of the domestic industry. 


d. Developments in technology: No evidence has been placed on record that the technology of 
the domestic industry is obsolete or that its product is not accepted in the market. 


e. Export performance: The information examined relates to the domestic sales of the 
domestic industry. Its export performance has been segregated. 


f. Productivity: Productivity per employee has declined since 2023-24. It has not prevented the 
domestic industry from earning profits. 


g. Performance of other products: The information examined is for the PUC alone. 


It follows that the moderation in the volumes and market share of the domestic industry in the period 
of investigation is attributable, to some extent, to the decline in demand and to competition from the 
other domestic producers. 


The Authority notes that in a sunset review the determination to be made is whether expiry of the 
duty is likely to lead to continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury. The fact that the domestic 
industry is not found to be suffering continued injury with the duty in force is a relevant fact, but 
does not conclude the review. The likelihood of recurrence of injury is examined hereinafter. 


J. MAGNITUDE OF INJURY MARGIN 


J. Views of the other interested parties 


00. 


0]. 


02. 


The following submissions have been made by the other interested parties with regard to the non- 
injurious price and the injury margin: 


Return on capital employed: The return of 22% on capital employed allowed in determining the non- 
injurious price is inflated and is not a reasonable return within the meaning of Annexure III. It 
originates in a drug price control order of 987, when interest and tax rates were much higher, and 
yields a far higher return on net worth. Reliance is placed on Bridgestone Tyre Manufacturing & Ors. 
v. Designated Authority and Hyosung Corporation v. Designated Authority (CESTAT) and on the 
practice of the European Union. The return actually earned by the domestic industry in a period 
without dumping should be adopted. 


Injury margin: On the basis of the invoices of HDTPL’s suppliers, the landed value of imports is 
above the non-injurious price, and the injury margin is negative. 


60 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


J.2 Views of the domestic industry 


03. 


04. 


05. 


The following submissions have been made by the domestic industry with regard to the non-injurious 
price and the injury margin: 


Return on capital employed: Annexure III provides for a reasonable return on average capital 
employed, and the Authority has consistently applied a pre-tax return of 22%. Guangdong Fuwa 
relies on hypothetical capital structures and does not point to any error in the verified cost or capital 
employed of the domestic industry. The practice of the European Union arises under a different 
statutory framework. 


Injury margin: The imports are entering below the non-injurious price, as they did in the original 
investigation and the first sunset review. 


J.3 Examination by the Authority 


06. 


07. 


08. 


The Authority has determined the non-injurious price for the domestic industry on the basis of the 
principles laid down in the Rules read with Annexure III, as amended. The non-injurious price has 
been determined by adopting the verified information relating to the cost of production for the period 
of investigation. For this purpose, the best utilisation of raw materials, of utilities and of production 
capacity over the injury period has been considered, and it has been ensured that no extraordinary or 
non-recurring expenses were charged to the cost of production. A reasonable return (pre-tax @ 22%) 
on the average capital employed (i.e., average net fixed assets plus average working capital) for the 
product under consideration has been allowed as pre-tax profit, as prescribed in Annexure III and as 
consistently followed by the Authority. 


As regards the return on capital employed, Annexure III provides for a reasonable pre-tax return on 
average capital employed for recovery of interest, corporate tax and profit. The return of 22% is a 
pre-tax return on capital employed, out of which interest on borrowings and corporate tax are to be 
met; it is not a return on net worth. It has been uniformly adopted by the Authority. The illustrative 
computations of Guangdong Fuwa rest on assumed capital structures and rates and do not relate to 
the capital employed of the domestic industry, and the practice of other jurisdictions under their own 
legislation does not govern the determination under Annexure III. The Authority further notes that, in 
the present case, the non-injurious price so determined is lower than the price actually realised by the 
domestic industry in the period of investigation. 


The landed value for the cooperating producer has been determined on the basis of its questionnaire 
response corroborated with DG Systems data. For the non-cooperating producers and exporters, the 
landed value has been determined on the basis of the facts available. Based on the landed value and 
the non-injurious price, the injury margin is as under:

K. LIKELIHOOD OF CONTINUATION OR RECURRENCE OF DUMPING AND INJURY 


K. Views of the other interested parties 
09. The following submissions have been made by the other interested parties with regard to the 
likelihood of continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury: 


a. Legal standard: A second sunset review does not justify automatic continuation of the duty. A 
forward-looking assessment must rest on objective and positive evidence. Findings in earlier 
proceedings are not conclusive, and anti-dumping duty is not intended to provide indefinite 
protection. 

b. Continued dumping and trend of imports: A dumping margin cannot be treated as conclusive of 
likely injury. Imports have fluctuated, and their continued presence does not show that expiry of the 
duty would lead to a significant increase in imports at injurious prices. 

c. Surplus capacity: The combined capacity of four Chinese producers cited by the domestic industry 
does not establish that Guangdong Fuwa has surplus capacity likely to be diverted to India. 
Guangdong Fuwa operates at a high rate of capacity utilisation. The mere existence of unutilised 
capacity or inventories does not establish likelihood. 

d. Exports to third countries: The existence of exports to third countries does not establish that such 
volumes would be directed to India or would enter at dumped and injurious prices. 

e. Condition of the domestic industry: The actual improvement in the performance of the domestic 
industry must be given due weight. The domestic industry cannot rely on the remedial effect of the 
duty as a substitute for establishing likelihood. 


K.2 Views of the domestic industry 


l0. The following submissions have been made by the domestic industry with regard to the likelihood of 
continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury: 


a. Legal standard: The test under Section 9A(5) of the Act and Rule 23(lB) is prospective and 
counterfactual. The “likely” standard applies to the overall determination, and the evidence must be 
assessed cumulatively. Reliance is placed on Rishiroop Polymers Pvt. Ltd. v. Designated Authority 
[(2006) 4 SCC 303], P.T. Asahimas Chemicals v. Designated Authority (CESTAT), US - 
Corrosion-Resistant Steel Sunset Review (WT/DS244/AB/R) and US - Oil Country Tubular Goods 
Sunset Reviews (WT/DS268/AB/R). 

b. Continued dumping and trend of imports: Dumping has continued despite nearly a decade of 
duty, with significant margins in every investigation. Chinese exporters retain an established 
presence in the Indian market. 

c. Surplus capacity: Four major Chinese producers, including Guangdong Fuwa, together hold 
unutilised capacity which is several times the Indian demand. The first sunset review had found the 
unutilised capacity of Guangdong Fuwa significant in relation to Indian demand. 

d. Exports to third countries: The Chinese producers are highly export-oriented. The first sunset 
review recorded, on Guangdong Fuwa’s own data, that it exported much larger volumes to third 
countries at prices lower than its prices to India. 

e. Circumvention: After the duty was imposed, the subject goods were imported in CKD/SKD form, 
and the Authority found that such imports undermined the remedial effect of the duty. This shows 
the commercial incentive to retain access to the Indian market. 

f. Condition of the domestic industry: The domestic industry has substantial idle capacity and is 
vulnerable. The imports are below its cost of sales, and on expiry of the duty it would have either to 
reduce its prices or to lose sales. 


62 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.!] 


g. Relevance of the first sunset review: Findings of an earlier sunset review remain relevant unless 


it is shown that the circumstances have changed (Association of Man-Made Fibre Industry of India 
v. Designated Authority, CESTAT). No such change has been shown. 


K.3 Examination by the Authority 


]. 


2. 


3. 


4. 


5. 


Section 9A(5) of the Customs Tariff Act, 975, read with Rule 23(/B) of the Anti-Dumping Rules, 
requires a forward-looking assessment of whether expiry of the existing duty is likely to lead to 
continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury. A sunset review does not require the domestic 
industry to show that it is presently suffering material injury while the duty is in force. Nor does the 
fact that duty has been in place for an extended period justify either its automatic continuation or its 
automatic expiry. The Authority has therefore examined the relevant factors together, rather than 
treating any one factor as conclusive. For this purpose, the Authority has considered the DG Systems 
transaction-wise import data, including post-POI information available on record, the verified 
information of the domestic industry, Guangdong Fuwa’s questionnaire response, the submissions of 
the interested parties and the findings of the previous investigations. The relevant evidence 
concerning import volumes and prices, available capacity, exports to third countries and the likely 
effect of expiry on the domestic industry is analysed below. 


Volume of imports from the subject country and continued dumping 

The subject goods continued to enter India at dumped prices during the period of investigation, 
despite the anti-dumping duty in force. As determined in paragraphs above, the dumping margins are 
*k*% for Guangdong Fuwa Heavy Industries Co., Ltd. and ***% for non-cooperating 
producers/exporters. This establishes continued dumping during the period of investigation. 


The volume of imports from China PR was ,279 MT in 2022-23, increased to 2,289 MT in 2023-24, 
and thereafter declined to ,48 MT in 2024-25 and ,352 MT during the period of investigation. 
Their share in Indian demand was ***%, ***%}, ***% and ***%, respectively. Thus, subject imports 
have maintained their presence in the Indian market despite the existence of anti-dumping duty. 


Post-POI developments 

The Authority has examined the DG Systems import data for the four-month post-POI period from 
October 2025 to January 2026. Subject imports from China PR, when annualised, work out to *** 
MT, marginally higher than the *** MT recorded during the POI. The landed value of the subject 
imports during the post-POI period remained broadly comparable to the POI landed value of 
approximately %,45,ll5 per MT, indicating no material increase in import prices. Thus, imports 
continued at a comparable annualised rate and at broadly unchanged prices despite the existing anti- 
dumping duty. While annualisation of four months’ imports is indicative and does not represent 
actual imports for a full year, the sustained import volumes and price levels, read with the dumping 
established during the POI and the available export capacity of Chinese producers, reinforce the 
strong likelihood of continued dumping and recurrence of injury if the duty expires. 


History of dumping and the earlier reviews 

The Authority notes that anti-dumping duty was imposed following the original investigation in 20l6 
and continued after the first sunset review in 202]. It was also extended to axles in CKD/SKD 
condition following the anti-circumvention findings of 202l, without attributing those findings 
individually to Guangdong Fuwa. This history reflects the persistent nature of dumped trade and the 
need for measures to address its effects. More importantly, dumping has continued during the present 
POI despite the duty, while Chinese producers retain substantial spare capacity, export orientation 


[भाग —ave [] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 63 


and access to the Indian market. Read with their comparatively lower third-country export prices and 
the likely price effects upon expiry, these circumstances provide strong evidence that dumping is 
likely to continue or intensify and material injury is likely to recur if the existing duty expires. 


Unutilised production and surplus capacities of major producers in China PR 


6. The domestic industry has furnished the following information regarding the production capacity, 
actual production and unutilised capacity of four major producers of the subject goods in China PR.

I7. The Authority notes that, as per the domestic industry's estimates, four major Chinese producers have 
a combined installed capacity of *** axles against production of *** axles, leaving substantial 
unutilised capacity of *** axles (approximately ***%). Considering an indicative weight of 350 kg 
per axle, the combined installed capacity and unutilised capacity are equivalent to approximately *** 
MT and *** MT, respectively, representing *** times and *** times the total Indian demand of 
approximately *** MT during the POI. Thus, the available surplus capacity alone is significantly 
higher than the entire Indian demand. Guangdong Fuwa's questionnaire response also confirms 
substantial spare capacity. The substantial available capacity, coupled with the export-oriented 
operations of Chinese producers, their established presence in India and the comparatively lower 
third-country export prices reported by Guangdong Fuwa, indicates both the ability and commercial 
incentive to increase exports to India. The Authority, therefore, considers that even partial utilisation 
or diversion of these capacities upon expiry of the existing duty could lead to a significant increase in 
dumped imports, establishing a strong likelihood of recurrence of material injury to the domestic 
industry. 
Available capacity and export orientation of Chinese producers 
I8. Guangdong Fuwa, the sole cooperating Chinese producer, has furnished the following information 


on its installed capacity, production and exports.

It is noted that: 


Guangdong Fuwa operated at high-capacity utilisation; however, its unutilised capacity of *** 
pieces during the POI exceeded the entire Indian demand and was many times its exports to India, 
showing substantial ability to increase supplies. 

Its exports to third countries remained significantly higher than exports to India and declined 
considerably over the injury period, indicating scope for diversion of export volumes to India upon 
expiry of the duty. 

Guangdong Fuwa’s export prices to third countries were consistently lower than its prices to India, 
at EEG REET EEG and ***% of the India price during 2022, 2023, 2024 and the POI 
respectively. This price pattern indicates a strong commercial incentive for lower-priced exports to 
be redirected to India. 

Guangdong Fuwa is the only cooperating Chinese producer. The domestic industry has also placed 
unrebutted information regarding substantial capacities of three other Chinese producers, indicating 
that significant additional supply capability exists in China PR. 

The Authority considers that the substantial spare capacity, high export orientation, lower third- 
country prices and established access to the Indian market, viewed cumulatively, demonstrate a 
strong likelihood of increased dumped imports upon expiry of the duty, resulting in downward 
pressure on domestic prices and recurrence of material injury to the domestic industry. 


Exports to third countries and attractiveness of the Indian market 

The information furnished by Guangdong Fuwa demonstrates a consistent and significant price 
differential between its exports to India and exports to third-country markets. In each of the periods 
reported, the average export price to third countries was materially lower than the corresponding 
export price to India. The third-country export prices were approximately ***%, ***%, ***% and 
*k*% of the prices realised on exports to India during 2022, 2023, 2024 and the POI respectively. 
This persistent price differential, read together with Guangdong Fuwa’s substantial export volumes 
and established presence in the Indian market, demonstrates that significant quantities are already 
being sold in other markets at prices materially below the prices charged to India. In the event of 
cessation of the existing anti-dumping duty, the additional price constraint presently operating in the 
Indian market would disappear, making India substantially more attractive for such lower-priced 


[भाग [खण्ड [] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 65 


2]. 


22. 


23. 


24. 


exports. The evidence therefore establishes a strong commercial incentive for diversion or redirection 
of exports from third-country markets to India upon expiry of the duty. 


The Authority further notes that Chinese producers have continued to maintain access to the Indian 
market notwithstanding the anti-dumping duty. This established market access, coupled with 
substantial available production capacity, high export orientation and materially lower prices 
prevailing in third-country export markets, demonstrates both the ability and the economic incentive 
of the subject-country producers to increase exports to India. The significance of the available 
capacity does not lie merely in its absolute magnitude but in the fact that even a limited diversion of 
the available exportable surplus would be substantial in relation to Indian demand. Accordingly, the 
combined evidence relating to capacity, export orientation, third-country pricing and existing trade 
flows strongly indicates that expiry of the measure is likely to result in increased exports to India at 
dumped and price-depressive levels. 


Likely import prices and price effects on the domestic industry 

The likely price effect of cessation of the duty has been examined by comparing the price position 
prevailing with the duty in force with the position that would prevail in its absence. During the POI, 
the landed value of subject imports, excluding anti-dumping duty, was approximately रें [,45,5 per 
MT, as against the domestic industry’s net sales realisation of approximately ?*** per MT and cost 
of sales of approximately =*** per MT. Thus, in the absence of the anti-dumping duty, the subject 
imports would undercut the domestic selling price by approximately %*** per MT and would also 
enter below the domestic industry’s cost of sales. The existing anti-dumping duty is therefore 
materially insulating the domestic industry from the full price effect of dumped imports. Further, 
Guangdong Fuwa’s exports to third countries have consistently been made at prices lower than its 
exports to India. If such lower-priced exports are redirected to India following expiry of the duty, the 
degree of price undercutting and price pressure would increase further. In addition, imports from 
non-cooperating producers have been found to land below the non-injurious price. The price 
evidence, therefore, clearly demonstrates that cessation of the duty is likely to expose the domestic 
industry to significant price undercutting, price suppression and price depression. 


Condition of the domestic industry and likelihood of recurrence of injury 

The Authority notes that the domestic industry has remained profitable and has recorded positive 
cash profits and returns during the period when the anti-dumping duty has remained in force. 
However, such improvement cannot be viewed in isolation from the remedial effect of the existing 
measure. The relevant consideration in a sunset review is whether such performance is likely to be 
sustained once the measure is withdrawn. In the present case, the domestic industry continues to have 
significant unutilised capacity and remains vulnerable to lower-priced imports. The landed value of 
subject imports without anti-dumping duty is not only below the domestic selling price but also 
below the domestic cost of sales. The difference between the domestic industry’s POI selling price 
and the landed value of imports without duty is also higher than the profit before tax earned by the 
domestic industry per unit. Consequently, any significant price reduction necessitated by intensified 
dumped imports would substantially erode, and is likely to eliminate, the profitability presently 
earned by the domestic industry. Alternatively, if the domestic industry seeks to maintain its prices, it 
is likely to suffer loss of sales volume and market share, resulting in lower production and capacity 
utilisation. Thus, the present improvement in the domestic industry’s performance reflects the 
operation of the existing remedial measure and does not diminish its vulnerability to recurrence of 
injury upon cessation of the duty. 


Overall assessment of likelihood of continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury 


The Authority has examined the likelihood factors cumulatively and notes that dumping has 
continued despite the existing duty; Chinese producers retain substantial production and unutilised 


66 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


25. 


26. 


| 8 


27. 


L.2 
28 


capacities, significant export orientation and established access to the Indian market; Guangdong 
Fuwa has exported substantial quantities to third countries at prices consistently lower than its prices 
to India; and, in the absence of duty, the landed value of subject imports would materially undercut 
the domestic industry’s selling price and remain below its cost of sales. These factors, taken together, 
demonstrate both the ability and incentive of Chinese producers to increase dumped exports to India 
upon expiry of the duty. 


The Authority further notes that expiry of the anti-dumping duty would remove the principal trade- 
remedial constraint affecting the competitiveness of Chinese exports and would make the Indian 
market significantly more attractive. Even diversion of a part of the available exportable capacity, 
particularly at the lower price levels observed in third-country markets, would be sufficient to exert 
substantial volume and price pressure on the domestic industry. 


Accordingly, the Authority considers that there is a strong likelihood of continuation or 
intensification of dumping and recurrence of injury if the existing anti-dumping duty is allowed to 
expire. Increased dumped imports at prices below the domestic industry’s selling price and cost of 
sales are likely to depress domestic prices, erode profitability, reduce sales and market share, and 
adversely affect production and capacity utilisation. The present improved performance of the 
domestic industry reflects the remedial effect of the existing measure and does not negate its 
vulnerability to recurrence of material injury upon cessation of the duty. 


L. INDIAN INDUSTRY’S INTEREST AND OTHER ISSUES 


Views of the other interested parties 


The other interested parties have made the following submissions with regard to the Indian industry’s 
interest: 


a. Public interest and impact on users: Continuation of the duty would not be in the public interest. 
The subject goods already bear basic customs duty. The duty raises the cost of axles for users and 
downstream industries and restricts the availability of competitive alternative sources. 

b. Duration of protection: Anti-dumping duty is not meant to provide indefinite protection. The 
domestic industry has enjoyed the measure for a considerable period and its performance has 
improved. 

c. Availability and competition: Seamless axles demanded by trailer manufacturers are not produced 
in India, Indian producers at times fail to meet the requirements of buyers, and the applicant is in a 
position to dominate the market. 


Views of the domestic industry 


. The domestic industry has made the following submissions with regard to the Indian industry’s 
interest: 


a. Public interest and impact on users: The impact of the duty on the price of a trailer is 
negligible. The duty does not prohibit imports; it only neutralises the unfair price advantage 
arising from dumping. Basic customs duty is payable irrespective of dumping and serves a 
different purpose. 

b. Duration of protection: The Act permits continuation of the duty for as long as the statutory 
test of likelihood is met. 


c. Availability and competition: The Indian producers have sufficient capacity to meet the entire 
Indian demand, and there is no demand-supply gap. 


[भाग [---खण्ड I] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 67 


L.3 Examination by the Authority

The Authority notes that the purpose of anti-dumping duty, in general, is to eliminate injury caused 
to the domestic industry by the unfair trade practice of dumping, so as to re-establish a situation of 
open and fair competition in the Indian market, which is in the general interest of the country. The 
imposition or continuation of anti-dumping duty does not restrict imports from the subject country in 
any way. The subject goods have continued to be imported throughout the life of the duty, and the 
availability of the product to consumers is not affected. 


The Authority issued the initiation notification inviting views from all interested parties, including 
importers, users and consumers, and prescribed a questionnaire for users to provide information on 
the possible effect of the duty on their operations. No user of the subject goods, and no association of 
users, has filed a response or opposed the continuation of the duty. 


The Authority notes HDTPL’s submission that continuation of the anti-dumping duty may increase 
import costs, restrict sourcing options and affect downstream competitiveness. However, HDTPL has 
not furnished any quantified evidence of the impact of the duty on its cost of production, profitability 
or downstream product prices; accordingly, the submission remains unsubstantiated in quantitative 
terms. 


The domestic industry has furnished an estimate of the impact of the duty on the price of trailers of 
three configurations. The Authority has re-worked the same at the rates of duty presently in force:

The incidence of the duty on the downstream product is thus insignificant. These estimates have not 
been disputed by any user, and no interested party has furnished any quantification to the contrary. 


There are six producers of the like article in India, which together supply the predominant share of 
Indian demand. The other Indian producers have increased their sales and market share during the 
injury period, which shows that there is active competition in the domestic market. There is no 
evidence of any shortage of the subject goods, of any type, during the injury period. 


As regards basic customs duty, the same is payable on all imports irrespective of whether they are 
dumped, and has been taken into account in the landed value for the purpose of the price comparison 
and the injury margin. It does not address dumping. 


M. POST-DISCLOSURE COMMENTS 


The Authority has examined the post-disclosure comments filed by interested parties and the 
domestic industry. The material submissions and the Authority’s findings are recorded below: 
Comments by the other interested parties 

i. Domestic industry and standing. HDTPL has claimed that the applicant is connected 
with Chinese producers through the SAF-HOLLAND group and common management. In 


68 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART I—SEc.]] 


support of this claim, it has referred to company reports, ownership records, transactions 
involving Qingdao YTE Special Products Co. Ltd. and SAF Holland (Yangzhou) Vehicle 
Parts Co. Ltd., and trade records. HDTPL has requested that the applicant be excluded from 
the domestic industry under Rule 2(b), that further details of these transactions be disclosed, 
and that the findings in the Wheel Loaders case be considered. 


ii. Bill of lading and trade records. HDTPL has referred to a shipping document from 209 
involving the applicant and Qingdao YTE. It has claimed that this document shows business 
dealings between the two companies, which were not properly examined in the Disclosure 
Statement. 


iii. Injury and likelihood. HDTPL has submitted that imports from the subject countries 
have declined and account for only a small share of Indian demand. It has further argued that 
the domestic industry's production, sales, profits and returns do not show any present 
material injury. Referring to Rule 5(3), safeguard provisions and the Soda Ash review, 
HDTPL has argued that anti-dumping duty should not be continued merely because imports 
may increase in the future. 


iv. Price undercutting. HDTPL has questioned the comparison of import prices with 
domestic prices in the Disclosure Statement. It has referred to selected sales invoices of the 
applicant and other Indian producers and calculated prices using estimated axle weights. 
Based on these calculations, HDTPL has claimed that imported goods are not being sold at 
prices lower than domestic goods (negative price undercutting). It has also requested that the 
lesser-duty principle be followed, meaning that the duty should not exceed the lower of the 
dumping margin and injury margin, and that the existing duty should not be increased. 


v. Non-cooperating exporters. HDTPL has stated that two Chinese exporters, namely 
Shijiazhuang Aotong Machinery Manufacturing Co. Ltd. and Shandong Darong Machinery 
Co. Ltd., did not submit their questionnaire responses. HDTPL has requested separate 
assessment of these exporters under Rule 22 and provisional assessment of their imports 
against a guarantee. It has also requested that these exporters be allowed to supply goods at 
agreed prices that do not cause injury to the domestic industry, through price undertakings 
under Rule I5. 


vi. Injury information and causation. HDTPL has questioned whether the applicant's 
reported profits relate only to the product under investigation or include profits from its other 
business activities. It has referred to the applicant's annual reports, increase in production 
capacity and identical index figures for production and capacity utilisation. HDTPL has also 
disputed the claim that imports have adversely affected domestic prices. It has requested that 
changes in demand and competition from other Indian producers also be examined as 
possible reasons for injury to the domestic industry. 


vii. Relief sought. HDTPL has requested the Authority to reconsider its findings, withdraw 
or reduce the existing anti-dumping duty, disclose further details of related-party 
transactions, and consider the requests for relief concerning the two Chinese exporters. 


Comments by the domestic industry 
i. Applicable review test. The domestic industry has submitted that, under Section 9A(5) and Rule 
23(/B), the Authority must examine whether dumping and injury are likely to continue or recur if the 
existing duty is removed. It has argued that improvement in the domestic industry's performance 


[भाग [---खण्ड [] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 69 


during the period of duty does not mean that the duty should be withdrawn. In support of its 
argument, it has referred to the Rishiroop Polymers and Indian Metal & Ferro Alloys judgments and 
relevant WTO reports on sunset reviews. 

ii. Product scope. The domestic industry has requested that the existing product scope covering non- 
driven trailer axles, including CKD/SKD forms covered under the anti-circumvention measure, 
should remain unchanged. It has opposed the exclusion of seamless axles merely because they are 
manufactured differently. It has also requested that observations regarding individual components 
should neither restrict the existing product scope nor affect the assessment of particular import 
consignments by Customs authorities. 

iii. Standing and procedure. The domestic industry has submitted that being part of a foreign group 
does not automatically make it ineligible under Rule 2(b). Referring to DG Systems data, it has 
denied that the applicant imported the product under investigation or that the alleged related Chinese 
company exported the product to India. It has supported the use of official import data and relevant 
information after the period of investigation (POI). It has also opposed the requests to terminate or 
reopen the earlier proceedings. 

iv. Normal value and dumping. The domestic industry has supported the calculation of normal 
value under paragraph 7 of Annexure I and the use of the cooperating exporter's verified export price 
after necessary adjustments. For exporters who did not cooperate, it has supported the use of 
available information under Rule 6(8). It has also disagreed with HDTPL's price comparisons based 
on invoices of other producers and different periods. The domestic industry has further relied on the 
positive dumping margins disclosed by the Authority. 

v. Injury and price effects. The domestic industry has submitted that, if the anti-dumping duty is 
removed, imported goods would be sold at prices lower than domestic prices and even below its cost 
of sales. Referring to the positive injury margins, it has argued that such low-priced imports would 
reduce its profits or result in loss of sales. 

vi. Likelihood and non-attribution. The domestic industry has submitted that dumping and injury 
are likely to continue or recur because imports are continuing, Chinese producers have substantial 
unused production capacity, exporters depend on overseas markets, and export prices to other 
countries are low. It has also referred to imports made after the POI. Further, it has requested that 
changes in demand and competition from other Indian producers should be examined separately 
while assessing injury. 

vii. Common expense allocation. The domestic industry has objected to the allocation of certain 
common factory expenses based on sales value instead of the prime-cost method used by it. It has 
requested that these expenses be allocated using a consistent and appropriate method, based on the 
nature of the expenses and supporting records. 

vili. Working capital. The domestic industry has requested that working-capital calculations should 
be based on the costs accepted by the Authority. It has also requested that deposits made with 
government authorities should be included wherever such deposits are necessary for the normal 
operations of the business. 


xX. Public interest and relief. The domestic industry has submitted that the existing anti- 
dumping duty has only a limited impact on trailer prices and does not stop imports. It has 
requested the Authority to maintain the existing product scope, confirm its eligibility as the 
domestic industry, and uphold the findings regarding the likelihood of continued or 
renewed dumping and injury. Accordingly, it has requested continuation of the anti- 
dumping duty for another five years.

M.3 
37. 


Examination by the Authority 
The Authority has examined the comments submitted by the interested parties and the domestic 
industry after the Disclosure Statement. The issues raised have been considered in light of the 
verified information and the findings already recorded. The Authority's examination of the main 
issues is given below. 


i. Domestic industry and alleged related-party imports 

The Authority notes that common ownership or management does not automatically make a 
domestic producer ineligible under Rule 2(b). The documents submitted by HDTPL indicate 
relationships between certain companies but do not establish that the applicant imported the subject 
goods from China PR or that the alleged related Chinese company exported these goods to India. The 
bill of lading relates to a shipment from India to China, while the other trade records do not establish 
imports or exports of the product under consideration. The official import data also does not support 
HDTPL's claims. Further, the facts of the Wheel Loaders case are different from the present case. 
Accordingly, the Authority finds no reason to exclude the applicant from the domestic industry under 
Rule 2(b). 


ii. Product scope and standalone components 

The Authority notes that the existing product scope covers non-driven axles for trailers, including 
axles in CKD/SKD, unassembled, unfinished or incomplete form. Seamless and welded axles are 
covered where their essential characteristics, functions and uses are comparable. However, the 
import of an individual component, such as a beam or tube, does not automatically mean that it is an 
axle in CKD/SKD form. Whether anti-dumping duty applies to a particular import shall be decided 
by Customs based on the nature of the imported goods and the applicable notification. Accordingly, 
no change in the existing product scope is warranted. 


iii. Legal standard applicable to the sunset review 

The Authority notes that the present sunset review is governed by Section 9A(5) and Rule 23(B). 
Under these provisions, the main issue is whether dumping and injury are likely to continue or recur 
if the existing duty expires. Therefore, the absence of present injury or an increase in imports is not, 
by itself, sufficient to justify withdrawal of the duty. The provisions relating to original 
investigations and safeguards, as referred to by HDTPL, do not change this requirement. The 
Authority notes that the Soda Ash sunset review was decided on different facts. In that case, 
dumping margins were negative or insignificant for most subject countries, the weighted-average 
injury margins were negative, and sufficient evidence of likely recurrence of injury was not 
established. In the present case, however, dumping has continued, positive injury margins have been 
determined, and the available evidence regarding spare capacity, third-country export prices and 
likely price effects indicates a likelihood of recurrence of injury. Accordingly, the findings in the 
Soda Ash case do not alter the conclusions reached in the present review. 

iv. Import trends, injury data and non-attribution 

The Authority has examined the official transaction-wise import data from DG Systems, which 
shows that imports from China PR continued despite the existing duty. Imports after the period of 
investigation (POI) have also been considered, but only as an indication of recent trends. Regarding 
the identical indices for production and capacity utilisation, the Authority notes that these are due to 
the installed capacity remaining unchanged and do not indicate any error. Further, the injury 
assessment is based on data relating to the product under investigation. Changes in demand and 
competition from other Indian producers have also been examined separately to ensure that any 
injury caused by these factors is not attributed to dumped imports. 


[भाग [---खण्ड I] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 7I 


v. Price undercutting and selected invoices 

The Authority has determined price undercutting by comparing the verified domestic selling 
price with the landed value of imports, excluding anti-dumping duty, on a comparable basis. 
The comparison shows positive price undercutting, indicating that imported goods were 
priced below the domestic selling price. HDTPL's calculation, which indicates negative price 
undercutting, is based on selected invoices and assumed axle weights and does not provide a 
comparable weighted-average price assessment for the period of investigation. Accordingly, 
the Authority finds no sufficient basis to revise the price undercutting determined in the 
Disclosure Statement. 


vi. Profitability and likelihood of recurrence of injury 

The Authority notes that the domestic industry has shown positive performance during the 
period when anti-dumping duty was in force. However, dumping continued during the POI. 
The available evidence also shows significant unused production capacity in China PR, 
exports to other countries at lower prices, and an existing market for Chinese exporters in 
India. These factors indicate that dumped imports are likely to increase if the duty expires. 
Such imports are likely to put pressure on domestic prices and adversely affect the domestic 
industry's performance. Accordingly, the Authority finds that expiry of the duty is likely to 
result in continuation of dumping and recurrence of material injury. 


vii. Normal value, export price and cooperation 

The Authority has calculated normal value under paragraph 7 of Annexure I, as explained in the 
earlier findings. The export price of the cooperating producer has been determined using its verified 
information after making the necessary adjustments. For producers who did not cooperate, the 
Authority has used the available information under Rule 6(8). The invoices submitted by HDTPL 
relate to other producers or different periods and do not provide a suitable basis for changing these 
calculations. Accordingly, the positive dumping margins disclosed earlier are maintained. 


viii. Requests under Rules 22 and 5 

The Authority notes that the two Chinese exporters named by HDTPL did not submit questionnaire 
responses in the present review. Merely claiming that an exporter did not export during the POI does 
not make it eligible for a new shipper review under Rule 22. Further, provisional assessment against 
a guarantee can be allowed only in a separate review under Rule 22, subject to the prescribed 
conditions and approval by the Central Government on the recommendation of the Designated 
Authority. 

Further, Rule 45, which deals with price undertakings, is not applicable to the present sunset review 
under Rule 23(3). In any case, no valid exporter-specific undertaking has been offered or examined. 
Accordingly, the requests made by HDTPL cannot be accepted in the present review. 


ix. Injury margin and continuation of duty 

The Authority has determined the non-injurious price and injury margins using the verified 
information and the method prescribed under Annexure III. These findings have been considered 
along with the evidence relating to the likelihood of future dumping and injury. In a sunset review, 
the Authority must examine what is likely to happen if the duty expires, rather than relying only on 
the injury margin during the period when duty was in force. Based on the evidence available, the 
Authority finds no sufficient reason to change the existing duty rates applicable to individual 
producers. Accordingly, continuation of the existing rates is considered appropriate.

x. Allocation of common factory expenses 

The Authority has examined the allocation of factory expenses while determining the cost of the 
product under investigation. Expenses directly related to the product have been considered 
separately, while common expenses have been allocated on a reasonable basis in accordance with 
Annexure III. The method of allocation must be appropriate to the nature of the expense and 
supported by the available records. The domestic industry has not provided sufficient verified 
information to establish that the method adopted by the Authority resulted in an incorrect cost 
calculation. Accordingly, no change is considered necessary. 


xi. Working capital and statutory deposits 

The Authority has calculated working capital for determining the non-injurious price based on the 
accepted costs and the assets and liabilities relating to the product under investigation. Regarding 
deposits with government authorities, the Authority notes that such deposits cannot be included 
merely because they appear in the company's accounts. Their inclusion depends on whether they are 
necessary for normal business operations and relate to the product under investigation. The domestic 
industry has not provided sufficient supporting calculations and records to justify their inclusion. 
Accordingly, the existing calculation is maintained. 


xii. Public interest and remaining requests 

The Authority has examined the concerns regarding the impact of anti-dumping duty on trailer 
manufacturers, availability of the product and competition in the Indian market. The evidence shows 
that other Indian producers also supply the product and does not establish any shortage caused by the 
existing duty. The estimated impact of the duty on the final price of trailers is limited, and no user 
has submitted verified calculations showing otherwise. Further, basic customs duty serves a different 
purpose and cannot replace anti-dumping duty. The existing measure also does not prohibit imports. 
The Authority therefore finds no sufficient reason to change its findings or accept the remaining 
requests, as they relate to earlier proceedings or matters outside the scope of the present review. 


N. CONCLUSION 


Having considered the investigation record, the disclosed facts and the comments received thereon, 
the Authority concludes as follows: 


The product under consideration and the existing CKD/SKD coverage remain unchanged. 
York Transport Equipment (India) Pvt. Ltd. qualifies as the domestic industry for the 
purposes of Rule 2(b). 


Dumping continued during the period of investigation despite the duty in force. The 
Authority has also found positive price undercutting in the absence of anti-dumping duty. 


The domestic industry’s positive performance under the existing measure does not remove 
the likelihood of injury on its expiry. The evidence concerning available capacity, export 
orientation, lower third-country prices, continuing imports and likely price effects 
establishes a likelihood of continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury. 


The Authority has examined other factors that may have affected the performance of the domestic 
industry. After considering the post-disclosure comments and the evidence on record, the 
Authority finds no sufficient reason to change its findings on dumping, injury and the likelihood 
of their continuation or recurrence. Accordingly, the Authority concludes that the requirements for 
continuation of anti-dumping duty under Section 9A(5) read with Rule 23(B) are satisfied. 


[भाग [---खण्ड I] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 73 


O. RECOMMENDATIONS 


39. The Authority has duly considered the oral, written and post-disclosure submissions of all interested 
parties, along with the verified evidence on record. Upon comprehensive examination, the Authority 
concludes that expiry of the existing anti-dumping duty is likely to result in continuation or 
recurrence of dumping and consequent injury to the domestic industry. 


40. Since the Authority has recommended continuation of the measure on the basis of the likelihood of 
continuation or recurrence of dumping and injury, it does not consider it appropriate to re-quantify 
the duty in force. Accordingly, the Authority recommends continuation of the definitive anti- 
dumping duty on imports of the product under consideration originating in or exported from China 
PR, at the existing producer-specific rates and country coverage specified in the duty table below, for 
a further period of five (5) years under Section 9A(5) of the Customs Tariff Act, 975, read with 
Rule 23 of the Anti-Dumping Rules, [995, unless revoked, amended or superseded earlier. The 
continuation shall be subject to notification by the Central Government.
